6.2
Ablation study

An ablation study was conducted on the two aforementioned questions. The following results support our inspection of
Section

4.2
and provide strong grounds for Theorem
3
.

Figure 3
:
(a) Optimal
\Delta_{\pi}^{*}
;
(b) Different forecaster
f
(ARIMA, SA)
;
(c) The Mean squared Error (MSE) model loss of
ProST-G
with
four
different forecasters (ARIMA and three SA) and the MBPO.
The
x
-axis in each figure shows the episodes.

Sub
optimal
\Delta^{*}_{\pi}
.
The experiments are performed over five different policy training times
\Delta_{\pi}\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
, aligned with SAC’s
number of gradient steps
G\in\{38,76,114,152,190\}
, under a fixed environment speed. Different from
our
theoretical analysis, we
set

\Delta_{t}=1
with
G=38
. We generate
o_{k}=sin(2\pi\Delta_{\pi}k/37)
,
which satisfies Assumption
1
(see Appendix
E.1
). The shaded area
s
of Figure
s

3

(a), (b) and (c)
are 95 % confidence area among three different noise bounds
of 0.01,0.02 and 0.03
in
o_{k}
. Figure
3
(a)
shows
\Delta_{t}=4
is close to the optimal
G^{*}
among five different choices.

Function
s

f,g
.
We investigate the effect of
the
forecaster
f
’s accuracy on the framework using two distinct functions: ARIMA and a simple average (SA) model, each tested with three different
the values of
w
. Figure
3
(b)
shows the average rewards of the SA model with
w\in\{3,5,7\}
and ARIMA model (four solid lines). The shaded area is 95 % the confidence area among 4 different speeds
\{1,2,3,4\}
. Figure
3
(c)
shows the corresponding model error. Also, we investigate the effect of
the
different model
predictor

g
by comparing MBPO (reactive-model) and
ProST-G
with
f=
ARIMA (proactive-model) in Figure
3
(c)
. The high returns from
ProST-G
with
f=
ARIMA, compared to those from MBPO
, empirically
support that the forecasting component of
the

ProST
framework can provide
a satisfactory
adaptability to the baseline algorithm that is equipped with. Also, Figure
s

3
(b)
and
3
(c)
provide empirical evidence that
the accuracy of
f
is contingent on the sliding window size, thereby impacting
the
model accuracy and subsequently influencing the agent’s performance.

7
Conclusion

This work offers the first study on the important issue of
time synchronization
for
non-stationary RL
. To this end, we introduce the concept of
the tempo of adaptation in a non-stationary RL,
and obtain a
suboptimal training time.
We
propose a Proactively Synchronizing Tempo (ProST) framework,
together with
two specific instances
ProST-T
and
ProST-G
.
The
proposed method adjusts an agent’s tempo to match the tempo of the environment to
handle
non-stationarity through
both
theoretical analysis and empirical evidence.
The
ProST framework provides a new avenue to implement reinforcement learning in the real world by incorporating the concept of adaptation tempo
.

As a future work, it is important to generalize the proposed framework
to learn a safe guarantee policy in a non-stationary RL by considering the adaptation tempo of constraint violations
[
25
,
26
]
. Another
generalization
is to introduce
an alternative
dynamic regret metric,
enabling a fair performance comparison among agents, even when they have varying numbers of total episodes.
Another
future work
is to find an optimal tempo of the distribution correction in offline non-stationary RL, specifically how to adjust the relabeling function to offline data in a time-varying environment that is dependent on the tempo of the environment
[
27
,
28
]
.

Appendix A
Details on Introduction

A.1
Experimental motivation

1. Environment details of 2D goal reacher

•

State space:
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S},|x|\leq 1,|y|\leq 1.

•

Action space:
\mathcal{A}=\{(\cos{(\pi/4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}k)},\sin{(\pi/4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}k)})~|~k=0,1{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},...,}7\}
(
|\mathcal{A}|=8
)

•

Reward function: if
the
agent’s state is in the Goal box, then
it
receives +6. Otherwise,
it
receives -0.5 rewards for every step.

•

Transition probability:
s_{h+1}=s_{h}+a_{h}\cdot\epsilon
, where

s_{h+1}
is
the
next state,
s_{h}
is
the
current state,
a_{h}
is the current action,
and

\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

with

||\epsilon||_{2}=1
provides a stocasticity to the environment.

•

Horizon length:
H=13

•

Discounting factor:
\gamma=0.99

•

Grid size: 10

•

Goal
box:
The
coordinates of the center
of the time-varying goal box
are

(x_{g},y_{g})
=(0.9\cos{(2\pi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}k/2500)},0.9\sin{(2\pi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}k/2500)})
, which
changes for episode
k\in[5000]
. The width and height
of the box are equal to
0.05
.

2. Experiment details

To motivate our proposed meta-framework
via
a simple experiment, we used Q-learning as a component
A
of our meta-algorithm to update the policy. The three baselines (ProOLS, ONPG, FTML) of Figure
1
(c)
were trained with four learning rates
\eta\in\{0.001,0.003,0.005,0.007\}

and the
entropy regularized parameter
\tau=0.1
,
where
the shaded area of the three baselines is 95 % confidence area among 4 different learning rates. The
PTM-T
was trained with
the
model rollout length
\widehat{H}\in\{50,60\}
, policy update iteration number
G\in\{10,50\}
, entropy regularized parameter
\tau=0.1
, Q
-
learning update parameter
\alpha_{Q}\in\{0.7,0.9,0.99\}
, and
the learning rate
\eta=0.001
. The shaded area of
PTM-T
is 95 % confidence area among the 12 different cases above. All four algorithms
share
the same agent’s policy network
structure
.

Appendix B
Related
W
orks

Existing methods for non-stationary environments can be grouped into three
categories
: 1) shoehorning: directly using established frameworks for stationary MDPs
by
assuming no extra mechanisms are needed since non-stationarity already exists in standard RL due to policy updates; 2) model-based policy updates: updating models with new data, using short rollouts to prevent model exploitation
[
24
,
29
]
, online model updates,
or
latent factor identification
[
4
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
; and 3) anticipating future changes by forecasting policy gradients or value functions
[
7
,
30
,
20
,
10
,
31
]
.

The advantage of the model-free method is its computational efficiency, allowing for direct learning of complex policies from raw data
[
32
,
33
]
, while the advantage of the model-based method is its data efficiency, allowing one to learn fast by learning how the environment works
[
34
,
35
]
. However, both advantages are weakened in non-stationary environments since the optimizing non-stationary loss function induced by time-varying data distribution makes the model-free method challenging to adaptively obtain the optimal policy
[
36
,
37
]
and the model-based method challenging to estimate accurate non-stationary models
[
20
,
10
]
.

Model-free method in non-stationary RL.

[
8
]
uses meta-learning among the training tasks to find initial hyperparameters of the policy networks that can be quickly fine-tuned when facing testing tasks that have not been encountered before. However, access to a prior distribution of training tasks is not available in real-world problems. To mitigate this issue,
[
9
]
proposed the Follow-The-Meta-Leader (FTML) algorithm that continuously improves an initialization of parameters for non-stationary input data. However, it internally entails a lag when tracking optimal policy as it maximizes the current performance over all the past samples uniformly. To alleviate the lag problem,
[
7
,
37
]

focused
on directly forecasting the non-stationary performance gradient to adapt the time-varying optimal policies. However, it still has problems of showing empirical analysis on bandit settings or a low-dimensional environment and lack of theoretical analysis which provides a bound on the adapted policy’s performance.
[
30
]
proposed adaptive Q-learning with a restart strategy and established its near-optimal dynamic regret bound. In addition,
[
36
]
proposed two model-free policy optimization algorithms based on the restart strategy and showed that dynamic regret satisfies polynomial space and time complexities. However,
the
provable model-free methods
in

[
30
,
36
]
still lack empirical evidence and adaptability in complex environments. Furthermore, since the agent
can
execute a policy in a fixed environment
only
once due to the non-
stationarity
of the environment, most existing model-free methods only update the policy once for each environment, which prevents the tracking of the time-varying optimal policies.

Model-based method in non-stationary RL.

The work

[
14
]

learned
the model change factors and their representation in heterogeneous domains with varying reward functions and dynamics. However, it
has restrictions for use
in non-stationary environments,
meaning that it is
applicable only for constant change factors or the domain adaptation setting.
[
4
]
proposed a Bayesian optimal learning policy algorithm by conditioning the action on both states and latent vectors that capture the agent’s uncertainty in the environment. Also,
[
15
]

brought
insights from recent causality research to model non-stationarity as latent change factors across different environments, and learn policy conditioning on latent factors of the causal graphs. However, learning an optimal policy conditioning on the latent states
[
4
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
makes the theoretical analysis
intractable.

The
recent works
[
20
,
10
,
31
]
proposed model-based algorithms with a provable guarantee, but their algorithms are not scalable for complex environments and lack empirical evaluation for complex environments.

Appendix C
Details on Problem
S
tatement and Notations

C.1
Details on Notations

Environment Interaction.

First, we denote
the
state and action at
the
wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
of step
h
as
s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}

and

a^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}
, respectively. As mentioned in the main paper, we
interchangeably use
the
symbols

s^{(k)}_{h}

and

a^{(k)}_{h}
for
s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}

and

a^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}
. At
the
wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
, the agent starts from an initial state
s_{0}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\sim\rho
. At step
h\in[H]
of the episode
k
, the agent takes
the
action
a_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}=\pi^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(\cdot|s_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}})
from the current state
s_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}
. The agent then receives the reward
r_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\sim R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},a_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}})
and moves to the next state
s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h+1}\sim P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s_{h+1}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}|s_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},a_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}})
. The trajectory ends when the agent reaches
s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{H}
.

Future MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{k+1}}
.

Our work
creates a
one-episode
-
ahead MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{k+1}}
based on the observed data from
the

p
lastest MDPs
\{\mathcal{M}_{t_{k-p+1}},...,\mathcal{M}_{t_{k}}\}
when the agent is stated in episode
k
. We define
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{k+1}}\coloneqq\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,\widehat{P}_{t_{k+1}},\widehat{R}_{t_{k+1}},\gamma\rangle
, where

\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}

and

\widehat{R}_{t_{k+1}}
are
the

forecasted
future transition probability and reward function, respectively. As mentioned in the main paper,
the agent also
interacts with the created future MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{k+1}}
in the same way as it did with the original MDP
\mathcal{M}_{t_{k}}
. We denote
the
state, action, and policy in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{k+1}}
as
\widehat{s}^{t_{k+1}}_{h},\widehat{a}^{t_{k+1}}_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{t_{k+1}}
,
or equivalently

\widehat{s}^{(k+1)}_{h},\widehat{a}^{(k+1)}_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}
, respectively. We elaborate our main methodology in Section
3
.

State value
and
state-action value function
s
.
For any given policy
\pi
and the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}
,
we
denote the state value function at
the

wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
(episode
k
) as
V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the state-action value function
k
as
Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We define

\displaystyle V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\gamma^{h}r_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}~\Big|~s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{0}=s\right],

\displaystyle Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\gamma^{h}r_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}~\Big|~s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{0}=s,~a^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{0}=a\right].

Also, given the future MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
, we denote the
forecasted
state value as
\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s):\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
forecasted
state-action value as
\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We define

\displaystyle\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\gamma^{h}\widehat{r}_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}~\Big|~\widehat{s}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{0}=s\right],

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\gamma^{h}\widehat{r}_{h}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}~\Big|~\widehat{s}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{0}=s,~\widehat{a}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{0}=a\right].

As mentioned in the main paper, we
simplify the symbols

V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}},Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}

as

V^{\pi,(k)},Q^{\pi,(k)},\widehat{V}^{\pi,(k+1)},\widehat{Q}^{\pi,(k+1)}
.

Dynamic regret.
Aside from stationary MDPs, the agent aims to maximize the cumulative expected reward throughout the
K
episodes by adopting a sequence of policies
\{\pi^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\}_{1:K}
. In non-stationary MDPs, the optimality of the
policies
is evaluated in terms of
the
dynamic regret
\mathfrak{R}\left(\{\pi^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\}_{1:K},K\right)

defined as

\mathfrak{R}\left(\{\pi^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\}_{1:K},K\right)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left(V^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(\rho)-V^{\pi^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(\rho)\right)

(C.1)

where
V^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(=V^{\pi^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\mathfrak{t}_{k}})

denotes
the optimal state value function
under
the optimal policy
\pi^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}
at the wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
(episode
k
) and
V^{\pi^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\mathfrak{t}_{k}}

denotes
the state value with agent’s
k^{th}
episode’s policy
\pi^{k}
. Dynamic regret is a stronger evaluation than the standard static regret
that
considers the optimality of
a
single policy over
all
episodes.

State value
and
state-action value
functions at
step
h
.

We denote the state value function
and the
state-action value function for any policy
\pi
at
step
h
of the wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
as
V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}

and

Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}
,
respectively. We define

\displaystyle V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\pi}\Big[\sum_{i=h}^{H-1}\gamma^{i-h}r_{i}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}~|~s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}=s\Big],

\displaystyle Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\pi}\Big[\sum_{i=h}^{H-1}\gamma^{i-h}r_{i}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}~|~s^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}=s,a^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}=a\Big].

Then, the corresponding Bellman equation is

Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s,a)=\big(R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}+\gamma P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}V_{h+1}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\big)(s,a),~~V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s)=\big<Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s,\cdot),\pi(\cdot|s)\big>_{\mathcal{A}},~~V_{H}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k},\pi}(s)=0~\forall s\in\mathcal{S}

(C.2)

where
\left(P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}f\right)(s,a):=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim P^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(\cdot|s,a))}[f(s^{\prime})]
for
every
function
f:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

We denote
V^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s)=V^{\pi^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}},\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s)
as the optimal state value function
at
step
h
of episode
k
. We omit the
subscript

h
when
h=0
, that is,
V^{\pi,k}=V^{\pi,k}_{0},~Q^{\pi,k}=Q^{\pi,k}_{0}
. Then, the corresponding
Bellman equation
is

\displaystyle Q^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s,a)=\big(R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}+\gamma

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}V_{h+1}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}\big)(s,a),~~V^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s)=\big<Q^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h}(s,\cdot),\pi^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(\cdot|s)\big>_{\mathcal{A}},

(C.3)

\displaystyle\pi^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s)=\max_{a}Q_{h}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a).

We also denote
the

forecasted
state value at
the
wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}
of step
h
when the agent
is stated
at time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
as
\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}
and
the

forecasted
state-action value as
\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}
in a forecasted MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
. We define

\displaystyle\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\pi}\Big[\sum_{i=h}^{H-1}\gamma^{i-h}\widehat{r}_{i}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}~|~\widehat{s}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}=s\Big],

(C.4)

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\pi}\Big[\sum_{i=h}^{H-1}\gamma^{i-h}\widehat{r}_{i}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}~|~\widehat{s}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}=s,\widehat{a}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}=a\Big].

(C.5)

Then, the
B
ellman equation is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,a)=\big(\widehat{R}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}+

\displaystyle\gamma\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}\big)(s,a),~~\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s)=\big<\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,\cdot),\pi(\cdot|s)\big>_{\mathcal{A}},

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{H}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s)=0~\forall s\in\mathcal{S}.

(C.6)

We
denote the
future
optimal policy of the
future
value function
\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
as
\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
. Then the
B
ellman equation also holds for
\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s)

and

\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s)
as follows
:

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,a)=\big(\widehat{R}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}+

\displaystyle\gamma\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}\big)(s,a),~~\widehat{V}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s)=\big<\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,\cdot),\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(\cdot|s)\big>_{\mathcal{A}},

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s)=\max_{a}\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s,a).

(C.7)

As mentioned in the main paper, we
simplify the notations

V^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h},Q^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{h},\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h},\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}

as

V^{\pi,(k)}_{h},Q^{\pi,(k)}_{h},\widehat{V}^{\pi,(k+1)}_{h},\widehat{Q}^{\pi,(k+1)}_{h}
.

Unnormalized (discounted) occupancy measure.

We define the unnormalized (discounted) occupancy measure
\nu^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{s_{0},a_{0}}\in\Delta_{1/(1-\gamma)}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})
at wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
(episode
k
) for
a
given policy
\pi

together with
an initial state
s_{0}
and
the
action
a_{0}
as

\displaystyle\nu^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{s_{0},a_{0}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{h}\mathbb{P}(s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a~|~s_{0},a_{0}~;~\pi,P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}})~~,~~\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

(C.8)

where
\mathbb{P}(s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a~|~s_{0},a_{0}~;~\pi,P^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}})
is the probability of visiting
(s,a)
at step
h
when following policy
\pi
from
(s_{0},a_{0})
with
the
transition probability
P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
.

We also define the unnormalized non-stationary (discounted)
forecasted
occupancy measure
\widehat{\nu}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{s_{0}}\in\Delta_{1/(1-\gamma)}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})
for
a
given policy
\pi
, an initial state
s_{0}
, an
action
a_{0}
, and
a
forecasted future transition probability
\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{s_{0},a_{0}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{h}\mathbb{P}(s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a~|~s_{0},a_{0},\pi,\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}})~~,\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

(C.9)

where
the
probability is defined in a forecasted environment with
\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}
.

Model prediction error.

To measure how well our meta-function predicts the future environment, we define two different
model prediction error
s
\iota^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\infty},\iota^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which denote the
Bellman
equation error when using
\widehat{V}

and

\widehat{Q}
estimated in the future MDP instead of the true
V

and

Q
function
s
:

\displaystyle\bar{\iota}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\infty}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}+\gamma P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}\widehat{V}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\infty}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\infty}\right)(s,a),

(C.10)

\displaystyle\iota^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}+\gamma P_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h+1}-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}},\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}\right)(s,a).

(C.11)

As mentioned
in the main paper, we allow
\bar{\iota}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\infty}(s,a)

and

\iota^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}(s,a)

to be interchangeably expressed by the symbols

\bar{\iota}^{(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)

and

\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)
.

Local time-elapsing variation budget.

Aside from the
time
-
elapsing variation budget, we define
the

local
time
-
elapsing variation budget
s

B^{(k-w:k)}_{p}

and

B^{(k-w:k)}_{r}
that quantifie how fast the environment changes
over
wall-clock time
s

\{\mathfrak{t}_{k-w+1},\mathfrak{t}_{k+1},...,\mathfrak{t}_{k}\}
where
k-w,k\in[K]
:

\displaystyle B^{(k-w+1:k)}_{p}(\Delta_{\pi})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\tau=k-w+1}^{k}\sup_{s,a}||P_{\mathfrak{t}_{\tau+1}}(\cdot~|s,a)-P_{\mathfrak{t}_{\tau}}(\cdot~|s,a)||_{1},

\displaystyle B^{(k-w+1:k)}_{r}(\Delta_{\pi})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\tau=k-w+1}^{k}\sup_{s,a}|R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(s,a)-R_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)|.

Appendix D
Proof of
T
heoretical
A
nalysis

D.1
Preliminary for ProST-T and theoretical analysis

In this subsection, we elaborate
on
the
ProST-T
’s environment setting and
its components

f,g
.

D.1.1
Environment setting

We consider the tabular environment
have the following properties:

1.

First,
P_{(k)}

and

R_{(k)}
are represented by the inner products of the feature functions
\psi:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|},\varphi:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
and the non-stationary variables
o^{p}_{(k)},o^{r}_{(k)}\in\mathcal{O}
, respectively, where
o^{p}_{(k)}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|}

and

o^{r}_{(k)}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
. That is,
P_{(k)}=<\psi,o^{p}_{(k)}>
and
R_{(k)}=<\varphi,o^{r}_{(k)}>
.

2.

Second, the agent estimates
o^{p}_{(k)}

and

o^{r}_{(k)}
rather than
observing them
. More specifically, we consider the non-stationary variable set
\mathcal{O}
to be the set
\{P_{(k)}\}_{1:K},\{R_{(k)}\}_{1:K}
. The agent then
attempts
to
estimate

o_{k}
(denote
P_{(k)}
as
o^{p}_{(k)}
and
R_{(k)}
as
o^{r}_{(k)}
) through its
w
lastest trajectories, where Assumption
2
does not
need
to be satisfied in this setting. That is, the agent estimates
P_{(k)}
by
\hat{o}^{p}_{k}
and
R_{(k)}
by
\hat{o}^{r}_{k}
from observations of last
w
trajectories,
i.e.,

\tau_{k-(w-1):k}
.

We elaborate on the above two
settings below:

1.
P_{(k)},R_{(k)}
are inner products of
\psi,\varphi
and
o^{p}_{(k)},o^{r}_{(k)}
.

Let us define a set of
one-
hot reward vectors over all states and the action space
, namely

\mathbbm{1}_{r}\coloneqq\{\varphi^{y}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}~|~\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}\varphi^{y}_{i}=1\}
, and
similarly define a set of
one-
hot transition probability vectors
, namely

\mathbbm{1}_{p}\coloneqq\{\psi^{y}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|}~|~\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|}\psi^{y}_{i}=1\}
.
We then define
one-to-one function
s

\varphi

and

\psi

such that

\varphi:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}_{r}

and

\psi:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}_{p}
. Namely,
\varphi(s,a)(\psi(s^{\prime},s,a))
is a
one-hot
vector
such
that the
(i)^{th}
entry equals 1. We
use
the notation
\varphi^{k}_{h}=\varphi(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})
for the observed
(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})
on the trajectory
\tau_{k}
, and
similarly

\psi^{k}_{h}=\psi(s^{(k)}_{h+1},s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})
.

Then, we set
\mathcal{O}=\{P_{(k)},R_{(k)}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
in
ProST-T
. Also, we set
o_{k}
to consist of two
parameters as

o_{k}=(o^{p}_{(k)},o^{r}_{(k)})
. We define a
function

o^{p}_{(k)}\coloneqq\{o:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|}~|~o(s^{\prime},s,a)=P_{(k)}(s^{\prime}|s,a),~\forall(s^{\prime},s,a)\}

and
a
function

o^{r}_{(k)}\coloneqq\{o:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}~|~o(s,a)=R_{(k)}(s,a),~\forall(s,a)\}
.
Then, the transition probability and reward value
P_{(k)}

and

R_{(k)}
can be constructed by the inner product
s
of the stationary function
s
\varphi
and
\psi
and the unknown non-stationary
parameters

o^{p}_{(k)}
and
o^{r}_{(k)}
as follows,

\displaystyle P_{(k)}(s^{\prime}~|~s,a)

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~<\psi(s^{\prime},s,a),o^{p}_{(k)}(s^{\prime},s,a)>\text{ for }\forall(s^{\prime},s,a),

(D.1)

\displaystyle R_{(k)}(s,a)

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~<\varphi(s,a),o^{r}_{(k)}(s,a)>\text{ for }\forall(s,a).

(D.2)

For
notational
simplicity, we
use

<\psi,o^{p}_{(k)}>

and

<\varphi,o^{r}_{(k)}>

to show the inner products of the
functions
\psi,o^{p}_{(k)}
and
\varphi,o^{r}_{(k)}
,
respectively.
Therefore,

P_{(k)}=<\psi,o^{p}_{(k)}>

and

R_{(k)}=<\varphi,o^{r}_{(k)}>
.

To give an
intuitive explanation,
note that

o^{p}_{(k)}
contains all transition probabilities for all
(s^{\prime},s,a)

in
a vector form with size
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|}
and
o^{r}_{(k)}
contains all rewards for all
(s,a)

in
a vector form with size
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
.

2. The agent estimates
o^{r}_{(k)}

and

o^{p}_{(k)}
rather than
observing
them

We have defined the
functions

o^{p}_{(k)}

and

o^{r}_{(k)}
as the transition probability and reward
functions
at episode
k
,
respectively. Now
,
the agent
strives
to estimate
o^{p}_{(k)}

and

o^{r}_{(k)}
, denoted as

\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k)}

and

\widehat{o}^{r}_{(k)}
, from
the current trajectory
\tau_{k}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k)}(s^{\prime},s,a)

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{(k)}(s^{\prime},s,a)}{\lambda+n_{(k)}(s,a)},~~\forall(s^{\prime},s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A},

\displaystyle\widehat{o}^{r}_{(k)}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\mathbbm{1}\left[(s,a)=(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})\right]\cdot r^{(k)}_{h}}{n_{k}(s,a)},~~\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

where
n_{(k)}(s,a)
denotes visitation count of state
s

under
action
a
through trajectory
\tau_{(k)}

and
n_{(k)}(s,a,s^{\prime})
denotes visitation count of state
s
under action
a
and subsequent next state
s^{\prime}
through trajectory
. We denote
\widehat{o}_{k,h}^{p}=\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k)}(s^{(k)}_{h+1},s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})
and
\widehat{o}_{k,h}^{r}=\widehat{r}^{k}_{h}(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})
.

It can be verified that the following relations hold at episode
k
for
the state and action
pairs from the
k^{\text{th}}
trajectory
\{s^{(k)}_{0},a^{(k)}_{0},s^{(k)}_{1},a^{(k)}_{1},..,s^{(k)}_{H-1},a^{(k)}_{H-1},s^{(k)}_{H}\}
:

\displaystyle P_{(k)}(s^{(k)}_{h+1}~|~s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})

\displaystyle~=~<\psi(s^{(k)}_{h+1},s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h}),o^{p}_{(k)}(s^{(k)}_{h+1},s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})>~~~~,\forall h\in[H],

(D.3)

\displaystyle R_{(k)}(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})

\displaystyle~=~<\varphi(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h}),o^{r}_{(k)}(s^{(k)}_{h},a^{(k)}_{h})>~~~~,\forall h\in[H].\

(D.4)

Note that
the observed non-stationary
parameters

\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k)}

and

\widehat{o}^{r}_{(k)}
can be interpreted partially observed
vectors
.

D.1.2
Functions
f
and
g

The function
f
estimates and
the function

g
predicts as follows

:

1.

Function
f
:
f
forecasts one-episode
-
ahead non-stationary
parameters

\hat{o}^{p}_{(k+1)}

and

\hat{o}^{r}_{(k+1)}
by minimizing the following loss function
\mathcal{L}_{f^{\diamond}}
with
the
regularization parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{f^{\diamond}}(\phi~;~\widehat{o}^{\diamond}_{(k-w+1:k)})=\lambda||\phi||^{2}+\sum_{s=k-w+1}^{k}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\left((\square^{s}_{h})^{\top}\phi-\widehat{o}^{\diamond}_{s,h}\right)

where
\diamond=r,p
and
\square=\varphi
if
\diamond=r
. We set
\square=\psi
if
\diamond=p
. We let
\phi^{k}_{f^{\diamond}}=\text{argmin}_{\phi}\mathcal{L}_{f^{\diamond}}(\widehat{o}^{\diamond}_{k-(w-1):k})
. We use
\phi^{k}_{f^{\diamond}}
as
\widehat{o}^{\diamond}_{k+1}
.

2.

Function
g
: Then
g
predicts the function
s

\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}

and

\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}
, denoted as

\widehat{g}^{P}_{(k+1)}

and

\widehat{g}^{R}_{(k+1)}
,
as
\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}=\widehat{g}^{P}_{(k+1)}\coloneqq<\varphi,\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k+1)}>
and
\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}=\widehat{g}^{R}_{(k+1)}\coloneqq<\varphi,\widehat{o}^{r}_{k+1}>+2\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}
, where

\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a):\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the exploration bonus term that adapts the counter-based bonus terms in the literature.

We elaborate on above two procedures
below:

1. The function
f
solves
an
optimization problem to
obtain
the future
\widehat{o}_{(k+1)}
.

The function
g\circ f
forecasts the
k+1^{th}
episode’s non-stationary
parameters
as
(\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k+1)},\widehat{o}^{r}_{(k+1)})
from
\widehat{o}_{(k-w+1:k)}
, where

w
is the sliding window length (past reference length). The function
f
forecasts
o^{p}_{(k+1)}

and

o^{r}_{(k+1)}
by minimizing the following two regularized least
-
squares optimization problems
[
18
]
.

\displaystyle\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k+1)}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{o\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}|\mathcal{A}|}}\left(\lambda||o||^{2}+\sum_{s=k-w+1,h=0}^{k,H}\left((\psi^{s}_{h})^{\top}o-\widehat{o}^{p}_{s,h}\right)\right)

(D.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{o}^{r}_{(k+1)}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{o\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}}\left(\lambda||o||^{2}+\sum_{s=k-w+1,h=0}^{k,H-1}\left((\varphi^{s}_{h})^{\top}o-\widehat{o}^{r}_{s,h}\right)\right)

(D.6)

2.
The
function
g
predicts
\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}

and

\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}
from
\widehat{o}_{k+1}
.

From the equations (17a) and (17b) of the paper
[
31
]
, the explicit solutions of (
D.5
) and (
D.6
)
are given as

\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k+1)}(s^{\prime},s,a)=\frac{\sum_{t=k-w+1}^{k}n_{t}(s^{\prime},s,a)}{\lambda+\sum_{t=k-w+1}^{k}n_{t}(s,a)},~\widetilde{o}^{r}_{(k+1)}(s,a)=\frac{\sum_{t=k-w+1}^{k}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\mathbbm{1}\left[(s,a)=(s^{t}_{h},a^{t}_{h})\right]\cdot r^{t}_{h}}{\lambda+\sum_{t=k-w+1}^{k}n_{t}(s,a)}.

(D.7)

Then, the
ProST-T
predicts the future model using the function
s

\widehat{g}^{P}_{k+1}

and

\widehat{g}^{R}_{k+1}
as follows
:

\displaystyle\widehat{g}^{P}_{k+1}(s^{\prime},s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq<\varphi(s^{\prime},s,a),\widehat{o}^{p}_{(k+1)}(s^{\prime},s,a)>,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}^{R}_{k+1}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq<\varphi(s,a),\widetilde{o}^{r}_{(k+1)}(s,a)>,

\displaystyle\widehat{g}^{R}_{k+1}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{g}^{R}_{k+1}(s,a)+2\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a).

We utilize the exploration bonus
\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a):\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

to
explore
those state and action pairs
that
are
less visited. We define it as
\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a)=\beta\left(\sum_{t=k-w+1}^{k}n_{t}(s,a)+\lambda\right)^{-1/2}

with

\beta>0
.
Then, we use
\widehat{g}^{P}_{k+1}

and

\widehat{g}^{R}_{k+1}

to denote
the future MDP’s
\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}

and

\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}
, respectively.
From the following analysis, we
write

\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}=\widehat{g}^{P}_{(k+1)}
,
\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}=\widetilde{g}^{R}_{(k+1)}
,
and

\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}=\widehat{g}^{R}_{(k+1)}
.

D.1.3
Baseline algorithm
s

Alg

and

\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}

The
ProST-T
utilizes softmax parameterization that naturally ensures that the policy lies in the probability simplex. For any function that satisfies
\theta:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the policy
\pi^{(k)}
is generated by the softmax transformation of
\theta^{(k)}
at
the
wall
-
clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
. Furthermore, to promote exploration and discourage premature convergence to suboptimal policies in a non-stationary environment, we
implement
a widely used strategy known as entropy regularization. We augment the future state value function with an additional
\pi^{(k)}(s)
entropy term, denoted by
\tau\mathcal{H}(s,\pi^{(k)})
, where
\tau>0
. We
perform
a theoretical analysis with two baseline algorithms : Natural Policy Gradient (NPG)
Alg
and Natural Policy Gradient (NPG) with entropy regularization
\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}

Softmax parameterization.

For any function that satisfies
\theta:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the policy
\pi^{(k)}
is generated by the softmax transformation of
\theta^{(k)}
at the wall
-
clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
.
Using
the notation
\pi^{(k)}=\pi_{\theta^{(k)}}
, the
soft parameterization is defined as

\pi_{\theta^{(k)}}(a|s)\coloneqq\frac{\exp{(\theta^{(k)}(s,a))}}{\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}{\exp{(\theta^{(k)}(s,a^{\prime}))}}}~~,\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}.

Under
the
softmax parameterization, the NPG update rule admits a simple form of update rule
given in
line 17 of Algorithm
2
in Appendix
F.1
. This is elaborated in
[
21
]
.

Entropy regularized value maximization.

For any policy
\pi
, we define
the

forecasted
entropy-regularized state value function
\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}+1}}_{\tau}(s)
as

\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\tau}(s)\coloneqq\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k}+1}(s)+\tau\mathcal{H}(s,\pi)

where
\tau\geq 0
is a regularization parameter and
\mathcal{H}(s,\pi)
is a discounted entropy defined as

\mathcal{H}(s,\pi)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{(k+1)}}\left[\sum^{H-1}_{h=0}-\gamma^{h}\log\pi(\hat{a}^{(k+1)}_{h}|\hat{s}^{(k+1)}_{h})|\hat{s}^{(k+1)}_{0}=s\right].

Also, we define the
forecasted
regularized Q-function
\widehat{Q}^{\pi,(k+1)}_{\tau}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{Q}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\tau}(s,a)=\hat{r}^{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{h}+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\widehat{V}^{\pi,\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}}_{\tau}(s^{\prime})\right]\\
&\text{where }(s^{\prime},s,a)=(\hat{s}^{(k+1)}_{h+1},\hat{s}^{(k+1)}_{h},\hat{a}^{(k+1)}_{h}).\end{split}

D.2
Notation for theoretical analysis

This subsection introduces some notations that we
will
use in the proofs.

At
the
wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
,
we
define the
forecasting model error

\Delta^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)
and
forecasting transition probability model error

\Delta^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)
below:

\displaystyle\Delta^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left|\left(R_{(k+1)}-\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}\right)(s,a)\right|,

(D.8)

\displaystyle\Delta^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left|\left|\left(P_{(k+1)}-\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}\right)(\cdot~|~s,a)\right|\right|_{1}.

(D.9)

Recall that
\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}

and

\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}

estimate the
future reward and transition probability by solving the optimization
problems
(
D.5
)
and
(
D.6
)
.

We define a
model error that considers the bonus term
as

\displaystyle\Delta^{Bonus,r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a)\coloneqq\left|\left(R_{(k+1)}-\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}\right)(s,a)\right|

where
\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}(s,a)=\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}(s,a)+2\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a)
.

We also define
the

empirical
forecasting reward model error
\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}

and the

empirical
forecasting transition probability model error
\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left|\left(R_{(k+1)}-\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})\right|,

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left|\left|\left(P_{(k+1)}-\widehat{P}_{(k+1)}\right)(\cdot~|~s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})\right|\right|_{1}

as well as
the
empirical
bonus
based on the
reward model error
:

\bar{\Delta}^{Bonus,r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}\coloneqq\left|\left(R_{(k+1)}-\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})\right|.

Likewise, we define

total empirical
forecasting reward model error
\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{K}

and the

total empirical
forecasting transition probability model error
\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{K}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{K}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h},

(D.10)

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{K}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}.

(D.11)

We simplify the symbols

\Delta^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a),\Delta^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a),\Delta^{Bonus,r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}(s,a),\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h},\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h},\bar{\Delta}^{Bonus,r}_{\mathfrak{t}_{k},h}

as

\Delta^{r}_{(k)}(s,a),\Delta^{p}_{(k)}(s,a),\Delta^{Bonus,r}_{(k)}(s,a),\bar{\Delta}^{r}_{(k),h},\bar{\Delta}^{p}_{(k),h},\bar{\Delta}^{Bonus,r}_{(k),h}
, respectively.

We also define
a
variable
\Lambda^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{w}(s,a)
that quantifies the visitation
:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{w}(s,a)=\left[\lambda+\sum_{t=(1\wedge k-w+1)}^{k}n_{t}(s,a)\right]^{-1}.

(D.12)

It can be verified that

\Gamma^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{w}(s,a)=\beta\sqrt{\Lambda^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{w}(s,a)}.

(D.13)

As before, we simplify the notations

\Lambda^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{w}(s,a)

and

\Gamma^{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}_{w}(s,a)

as

\Lambda^{(k)}_{w}(s,a)

and

\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a)
. We define
r_{\text{max}},\widetilde{r}_{\text{max}},R_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}
, and
\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}
as follows
:

\displaystyle R_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{(s,a)}|R_{(k+1)}(s,a)|,

\displaystyle r_{\text{max}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{1\leq k\leq K-1}R_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{(s,a)}|\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}(s,a)|,

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{\text{max}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{1\leq k\leq K-1}\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}

and since
||\widehat{R}_{(k+1)}(s,a)||_{\infty}\leq||\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}(s,a)||_{\infty}+||2\Gamma^{(k)}_{w}(s,a)||_{\infty}=\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}+\frac{2\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda}}
, we define
\hat{r}^{k+1}_{\text{max}}
as

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{(k+1)}^{\text{max}}+\frac{2\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda}}.

Also, since
\beta

and

\lambda
are hyperparameters independent of
k
,
we have that

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{\text{max}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{r}_{\text{max}}+\frac{2\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda}}.

(D.14)

D.3
Proofs

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Following the definition of the dynamic regret (Definition
C.1
)
, it
can be separated into three terms
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}\left(\{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}\}_{1:K-1},K)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left(V^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left(V^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)}_{\hbox to7.83pt{\vbox to7.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.91264pt\lower-3.91264pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}

1. Upper bound on
1
.

The gap between
V^{\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})
and
\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})
comes from the gap
between
two optimal value functions evaluated
for
two different MDPs:
\mathcal{M}_{(k+1)}

and

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{(k+1)}
.

We
will
first come up with
an
upper bound
on
the difference between
Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)
and
\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)
for any
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
. The difference can be separated into three terms as follows:

\displaystyle Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{||Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)-Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)||_{\infty}}_{\hbox to12.91pt{\vbox to12.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.45293pt\lower-6.45293pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.65 0 C 8.65 4.78 4.78 8.65 0 8.65 C -4.78 8.65 -8.65 4.78 -8.65 0 C -8.65 -4.78 -4.78 -8.65 0 -8.65 C 4.78 -8.65 8.65 -4.78 8.65 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.11807pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left(Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)\right)}_{\hbox to12.91pt{\vbox to12.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.45293pt\lower-6.45293pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\displaystyle+\underbrace{||\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)||_{\infty}}_{\hbox to12.91pt{\vbox to12.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.45293pt\lower-6.45293pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

1.1. Term
s

1.1
and
1.3
.

First, the term
1.1
can be bounded as follows
:

1.1

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast}}\Big[\sum_{i=0}^{H-h-1}\gamma^{i}r_{i+h}^{(k+1)}-\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{i}^{(k+1)}~|~s^{(k+1)}_{h}=s,a^{(k+1)}_{h}=a\Big]\right|\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{i=H-h}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{\text{max}}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma^{H-h}}{1-\gamma}r_{\text{max}}

Through a similar process, we can also obtain the upper bound
:

\hbox to16.01pt{\vbox to16.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.00322pt\lower-8.00322pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.8 0 C 10.8 5.96 5.96 10.8 0 10.8 C -5.96 10.8 -10.8 5.96 -10.8 0 C -10.8 -5.96 -5.96 -10.8 0 -10.8 C 5.96 -10.8 10.8 -5.96 10.8 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.3889pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\leq\gamma^{H-h}/(1-\gamma)\hat{r}_{\text{max}}
.

1.2. Term
1.2
.

An
upper bound
on
the term
1.2
can be
obtained by
utilizing
\bar{\iota}^{(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)
(Def (
C.10
)). Then, the Q-function gap between
Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}

and

\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}
can be represented using the
Bellman
equation as follows
:

1.2

\displaystyle=\left(Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}\right)(s,a)

(D.15)

\displaystyle=\big(R_{(k+1)}+\gamma P_{(k+1)}V_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\big)(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)

(D.16)

\displaystyle=\big(R_{(k+1)}+\gamma P_{(k+1)}\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}\big)(s,a)+\gamma P_{(k+1)}\left(V_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)+\gamma P_{(k+1)}\left(V_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{h}(s,a)+\gamma P_{(k+1)}\left(\langle Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}-\langle\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}\right)(s,a)

(D.17)

\displaystyle=\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)+\gamma P_{(k+1)}\big(\langle Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle+\langle\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}\big)(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)+\gamma P_{(k+1)}\left(\langle Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}\right)(s,a)

(D.18)

where (
D.16
) and (
D.17
) hold by the definition of
Bellman
equation ((
C.3
) and (
C.7
)). Equation (
D.18
) holds by
\langle\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}(s,a)\leq 0
since
\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}
is the optimal policy of
\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}
.
We now define the matrix operator
(\mathbb{P}\circ\pi)(s,a):\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
as the transition matrix
that captures how the
state
-
action pair transition
s
from
(s,a)
to
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
when following the policy
\pi
in an environment with
the
transition probability
\mathbb{P}
. Also, define the one-vector
\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{A}|}

such
that the
(s,a)^{\text{th}}
entity is one and
the remaining entries are
zero. Then
,
the equation (
D.15
)
becomes
the same as the
(s,a)^{\text{th}}
entity of the vector
\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot(Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)})(s,a)
. Also, the
right-hand side
of equation (
D.18
) can be represented as

\displaystyle P_{(k+1)}\left(\langle Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)},\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=(P_{(k+1)}\circ\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)})

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot(Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)})\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{P}^{k+1}_{\pi^{\bigast}})\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot(Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)})\right)(s,a)

where we denote
P_{(k+1)}\circ\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}}
for
notational
simplicity.

Then, we can reformulate the inequality (between (
D.15
) and (
D.18
)) into a vector form
which
holds element-wise for all
s,a
:

\displaystyle\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot\left(Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}\right)\right)(s,a)\leq

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot\bar{\iota}^{(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)

\displaystyle+\gamma(\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}})\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot(Q_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)})\right)(s,a)

Then, rearranging the above inequality yields
that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot\left(Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq(\mathbb{I}-\gamma\mathbb{P}^{k+1}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)

(D.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)

Now, note that
(\mathbb{I}-\gamma\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{-1}
can be expanded with
an
infinite summation of
the
matrix operator
P_{(k+1)}\circ\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)}
as
(\mathbb{I}-\gamma\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\gamma\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}}+(\gamma\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{2}+...
s. Since
,
\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}
can be viewed as the Dirac delta state
-
action distribution that always yields
(s,a)
,
it holds that

\nu_{(s,a)}^{\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}=(\mathbb{I}-\gamma\mathbb{P}^{(k+1)}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}
, where
\nu
is the unnormalized occupancy measure of
(s,a)

in light of
Definition (
C.8
).
Then taking the
l_{1}
norm over the inequality (
D.19
) yields the
that

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot\left(Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\infty}\right)(s,a)\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|(\mathbb{I}-\gamma\mathbb{P}^{k+1}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\cdot\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)\right|\right|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|(\mathbb{I}-\gamma\mathbb{P}^{k+1}_{\pi^{\bigast}})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{(s,a)}\right|\right|_{1}\cdot\left|\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left|\bar{\iota}^{k+1}_{\infty}(s,a)\right|

(D.20)

Equation (
D.20
) holds since
\nu_{(s,a)}^{\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}
is
an
unnormalized probability distribution.

Then, for
every

(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times[H]
,
it follows from
combining the terms
\hbox to16.01pt{\vbox to16.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.00322pt\lower-8.00322pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.8 0 C 10.8 5.96 5.96 10.8 0 10.8 C -5.96 10.8 -10.8 5.96 -10.8 0 C -10.8 -5.96 -5.96 -10.8 0 -10.8 C 5.96 -10.8 10.8 -5.96 10.8 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.3889pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\hbox to16.01pt{\vbox to16.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.00322pt\lower-8.00322pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.8 0 C 10.8 5.96 5.96 10.8 0 10.8 C -5.96 10.8 -10.8 5.96 -10.8 0 C -10.8 -5.96 -5.96 -10.8 0 -10.8 C 5.96 -10.8 10.8 -5.96 10.8 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

and
1.3

that

Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{h}(s,a)\leq\frac{\gamma^{H-h}}{1-\gamma}(r_{\text{max}}+\hat{r}_{\text{max}})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left|\bar{\iota}^{(k+1)}_{\infty}(s,a)\right|

1.3. Combining the terms
\hbox to17.72pt{\vbox to17.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.85893pt\lower-8.85893pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\hbox to17.72pt{\vbox to17.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.85893pt\lower-8.85893pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.98 0 C 11.98 6.62 6.62 11.98 0 11.98 C -6.62 11.98 -11.98 6.62 -11.98 0 C -11.98 -6.62 -6.62 -11.98 0 -11.98 C 6.62 -11.98 11.98 -6.62 11.98 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.34718pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
and
1.3
.

Finally,
an
upper bound
on

1
is
derived as

1

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left(V^{\pi^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left|\left|Q^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}\right|\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\cdot\frac{\gamma^{H}}{1-\gamma}(r_{\text{max}}+\hat{r}_{\text{max}})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}||\bar{\iota}_{\infty}^{k+1}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle=(K-1)\cdot\frac{\gamma^{H}}{1-\gamma}(r_{\text{max}}+\hat{r}_{\text{max}})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\bar{\iota}_{\infty}^{K}

(D.21)

where we have defined
\bar{\iota}_{\infty}^{K}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left|\left|\bar{\iota}_{\infty}^{(k+1)}\right|\right|_{\infty}
in
Theorem

1
.

2. Upper bound
on

2
.

The gap between
\widehat{V}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})
and
\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})
comes from
the
optimization error between
the
optimal policy
\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}
and
the
policy
\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}
, which
are both driven from
the
same MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{(k+1)}
. We also separate
this
gap into three terms
:

\displaystyle\hbox to9.93pt{\vbox to9.93pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-4.9644pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{'s}~(k)^{\text{th}}~\text{term}

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{V}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)+\left(\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)+

\displaystyle+\left(\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)

(D.22)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left(\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)}_{\hbox to12.91pt{\vbox to12.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.45293pt\lower-6.45293pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.65 0 C 8.65 4.78 4.78 8.65 0 8.65 C -4.78 8.65 -8.65 4.78 -8.65 0 C -8.65 -4.78 -4.78 -8.65 0 -8.65 C 4.78 -8.65 8.65 -4.78 8.65 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.11807pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\frac{2\gamma^{H}\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}}{1-\gamma}

(D.23)

where
the subscript

\infty
in the
notations

\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\pi,(k+1)}(s_{0})

and

\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\pi,(k+1)}(s_{0})

indicate the
forecasted value function
and the

(infinite horizon MDPs). Equation (
D.22
) holds since
\widehat{V}^{\pi,(k+1)}(s)-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\pi,(k+1)}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{(k+1)},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=H}^{\infty}\gamma^{h}\widehat{r}_{h}^{(k+1)}~\Big|~s=\widehat{s}^{(k+1)}_{0}\right]\leq\frac{\gamma^{H}}{1-\gamma}\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}
holds for
all

\pi\in\Pi
.

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\log{|\mathcal{A}|}}{\eta G}+\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{2}G}

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})

\displaystyle\leq(K-1)\left(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{2}G}+\frac{\log{|\mathcal{A}|}}{\eta G}+\frac{2\gamma^{H}\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}}{1-\gamma}\right)

(D.25)

2.2. Upper bound
on

2
- NPG with entropy regularization (
\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}
).

The
term
2.1
in (
D.23
) can be further bounded as follows
:

\displaystyle\hbox to16.01pt{\vbox to16.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.00322pt\lower-8.00322pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.3889pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)+\left(\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})\right|\right|_{\infty}+\left|\left|\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right|\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right|\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left|\left|\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\bigast,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right|\right|_{\infty}}_{\hbox to12.91pt{\vbox to12.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.45293pt\lower-6.45293pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.11807pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\frac{2\tau\log|\mathcal{A}|}{1-\gamma}

(D.26)

where (
D.26
) holds since
\left|\left|\widehat{V}_{\infty}^{\pi,(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}_{\infty,\tau}^{\pi,(k+1)}(s_{0})\right|\right|_{\infty}=\tau\max_{s}\left|\mathcal{H}(s,\pi)\right|\leq\tau\frac{\log\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}{1-\gamma}
holds for
all

\pi
.

We now bound
the term
2.2

in
(
D.26
). With the policy-update rule of
ProST-T
(Algorithm
2
in Appendix
F.2
), suppose
that
for
a
given
g\in[\Delta_{\pi}]
, we
have obtained an

inexact
soft
Q
-function value of the policy
\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}
as
\widetilde{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}_{\tau}
, where

\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}_{\tau}
denotes
an

exact
soft forecated
Q
-function value
and

g
is the iteration index. The approximation gap
|\widetilde{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}_{\tau}-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}_{\tau}|

results from
computing
Q
using
a finite number of samples
. For
a
hyperparameter
\delta
,
let
the maximum of
the
approximation gap over
(s,a)
is smaller than
\delta
, namely
||\widetilde{Q}_{\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}-\widehat{Q}_{\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}||_{\infty}\leq\delta
holds. Then, for iteration
g=1,2,..,\Delta_{\pi}
, the policy-update rule of
ProST-T
can be written
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}_{(g+1)}(\cdot|s)=\frac{1}{Z_{(g)}}\cdot\left(\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}(\cdot|s)\right)^{1-\frac{\eta\tau}{1-\gamma}}\exp{\left(\frac{\eta\widetilde{Q}_{\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}(s,a)}{1-\gamma}\right)}

\displaystyle\text{where}~~||\widetilde{Q}_{\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}_{\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}(s,a)||_{\infty}\leq\delta~~\text{for}~~\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

where
Z_{(g)}(s)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}(a|s)\right)^{1-\frac{\eta\tau}{1-\gamma}}\exp{\left((\eta\widehat{Q}_{\tau}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}(s,a))/(1-\gamma)\right)}
.

In light of
Theorem 2
in

[
21
]
, when the learning rate
is such that

0\leq\eta\leq(1-\gamma)/\tau
, then the approximate entropy-regularized NPG method satisfies the linear convergence theorem for
every

g\in[\Delta_{\pi}]
:

||\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\tau}-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}}_{\tau}||_{\infty}\leq\gamma\left[(1-\eta\tau)^{g-1}C_{1}+C_{2}\right]

(D.27)

||\log\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\log\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}||_{\infty}\leq 2\tau^{-1}\left[(1-\eta\tau)^{g-1}C_{1}+C_{2}\right]

(D.28)

where

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle:=||\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\tau}-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(0)}}_{\tau}||_{\infty}+2\tau\left(1-\frac{\eta\tau}{1-\gamma}\right)||\log\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\log\widehat{\pi}_{(0)}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle=||\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\tau}-\widehat{Q}^{\pi^{(k)}}_{\tau}||_{\infty}+2\tau\left(1-\frac{\eta\tau}{1-\gamma}\right)||\log\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\log\widehat{\pi}^{(k)}||_{\infty}

(D.29)

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2\delta}{1-\gamma}\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\eta\tau}\right)

(D.30)

The equation (
D.29
) holds since the policy that the agent executes at the wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
(episode
k
), i.e.
,

\pi^{(k)}
, is same as the initial policy of the policy iteration, i.e.
,

\widehat{\pi}_{(0)}
,
at the wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k}
. Also, the policy that the agent executes at the wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}
, i.e.
,

\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}
, is same as the policy after
\Delta_{\pi}
steps of
the
soft policy iteration, i.e.
,

\widehat{\pi}_{(\Delta_{\pi})}
at the wall-clock time
\mathfrak{t}_{k+1}
.

Now
, the term
2.2
can be bounded as follows
:

2.2

\displaystyle=||\widehat{V}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\tau}-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}}_{\tau}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle=||\widehat{V}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\tau}-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(\Delta_{\pi})}}_{\tau}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq||\widehat{Q}^{\bigast,(k+1)}_{\tau}-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}_{(\Delta_{\pi})}}_{\tau}||_{\infty}+\tau||\log\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)}-\log\widehat{\pi}_{(g)}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\gamma+2\right)\left[(1-\eta\tau)^{\Delta_{\pi}-1}C_{1}+C_{2}\right]

(D.31)

Combining (
D.23
,
D.26
and
D.31
) offers
an
upper bound
on
the term
2
’s
k^{(th)}
term as follows,

\displaystyle\hbox to9.93pt{\vbox to9.93pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-4.9644pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.59 0 C 6.59 3.64 3.64 6.59 0 6.59 C -3.64 6.59 -6.59 3.64 -6.59 0 C -6.59 -3.64 -3.64 -6.59 0 -6.59 C 3.64 -6.59 6.59 -3.64 6.59 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{'s}~(k)^{th}~\text{term}

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\gamma+2\right)\left[(1-\eta\tau)^{\Delta_{\pi}-1}C_{1}+C_{2}\right]+\frac{2\gamma^{H}\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}}{1-\gamma}+\frac{2\tau\log|\mathcal{A}|}{1-\gamma}

(D.32)

Hence,

2

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left(\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\bigast,(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})-\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq(K-1)\left(\left(\gamma+2\right)\left[(1-\eta\tau)^{\Delta_{\pi}-1}C_{1}+C_{2}\right]+\frac{2\gamma^{H}\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}}{1-\gamma}+\frac{2\tau\log|\mathcal{A}|}{1-\gamma}\right)

(D.33)

where (
D.32
) and (
D.33
)
hold
when
0\leq\eta\leq(1-\gamma)/\tau

3. Upper bound
on

3
.

By recalling
Definition (
C.11
), note that
\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(\widehat{s}^{(k+1)}_{h},\widehat{a}^{(k+1)}_{h})
is
an

empirical
estimated model prediction error,
measuring
the gap between
\mathcal{M}_{(k+1)}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{(k+1)}
. Specifically, at episode
k
, the
ProST
algorithm creates the future MDP
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{(k+1)}
and evaluates
\widehat{V}

and

\widehat{Q}
using
\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}
. Subsequently
at episode
k+1
, the agent uses
\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}
to rollout a trajectory
\{s^{(k+1)}_{0},a^{(k+1)}_{0},s^{(k+1)}_{1},a^{(k+1)}_{1},...,s^{(k+1)}_{H-1},a^{(k+1)}_{H-1},s^{(k+1)}_{H}\}
.
Based on this observation, one can write

\displaystyle\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})=

\displaystyle R_{(k+1)}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})+\gamma(P_{(k+1)}\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1})(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{(k+1)}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})+\gamma(P_{(k+1)}\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1})(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})+Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma P_{(k+1)}(\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1})(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle+Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

(D.34)

Equation (
D.34
) holds
due to
(
C.1
). Now,
we
define the operator
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}^{(k+1)}
for
a
function
f:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows
:

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}^{(k+1)}f)(s)\coloneqq\langle f(s,\cdot),\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}(\cdot|s)\rangle_{\mathcal{A}}

Recall that
\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s)=\big<\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}\big>_{\mathcal{A}}
and
V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s)=\big<Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}\big>_{\mathcal{A}}

in light of
(
C.1
) and (
C.2
). Then, the gap between
\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s^{(k+1)}_{h})
and
V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}(s^{(k+1)}_{h})
can be expanded as

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s^{(k+1)}_{h})-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}^{(k+1)}\left(\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}\right)\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}^{(k+1)}\left(\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}\right)\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h})-\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle\text{ }+\gamma P_{(k+1)}(\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1})(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle\text{ }+\left(Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}-\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

Now, we define two sequences
\{D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}\}

and

\{D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}\}

, where

(k,h)=(0,0),(0,1),...,(K-1,H)
. We define
D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}

and

D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}
as

\displaystyle D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\gamma^{h}\left(\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}^{(k+1)}\left(\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}\right)\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle-\gamma^{h}\left(\widehat{Q}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\gamma^{h+1}P_{(k+1)}(\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1})(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle-\gamma^{h+1}\left(\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h+1})

Therefore, we have the following recursive formula over
h
:

\displaystyle\gamma^{h}\left(\widehat{V}_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h}\right)(s^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle=D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}+D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}+\gamma^{h+1}\left(\widehat{V}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{h+1}\right)\left(s^{(k+1)}_{h+1}\right)-\gamma^{h}\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

The
summation over
h=0,1,..,H-1
yields that

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{0}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}(s^{(k+1)}_{0})-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}_{0}(s^{(k+1)}_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\left(D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}+D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}\right)-\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\gamma^{h}\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h}).

Now, for every
(k,h)\in[K]\times[H]
, we define
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{h,1}
as a
\sigma-
algebra generated by state-action sequences
\{(s^{\tau}_{i},a^{\tau}_{i})\}_{(\tau,i)\in[k-1]\times[H]}\cup\{(s_{i}^{k},a_{i}^{k})\}_{i\in[h]}
and define
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{h,2}
as
a

\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{(s^{\tau}_{i},a^{\tau}_{i})\}_{(\tau,i)\in[k-1]\times[H]}\cup\{(s_{i}^{k},a_{i}^{k})\}_{i\in[h]}\cup\{s^{(k)}_{h+1}\}
. A filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{h,m}\}_{(k,h,m)\in[K]\times[H]\times[2]}
is a sequence of
\sigma
- algebras in terms of the time index
t(k,h,m)=2(k-1)H+2h+m
such that
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{h,m}\subset\mathcal{F}^{k^{\prime}}_{h^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
for every
t(k,h,m)\leq t((k^{\prime}),h^{\prime},m^{\prime})
. The
estimates

\widehat{V}^{\pi,(k+1)}_{h}

and

\widehat{Q}^{\pi,(k+1)}_{h}
are
\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}_{1,1}
measurable since they are forecasted from
the
past
k
historical trajectories. Now, since
D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}\in\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}_{h,1}
and
D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}\in\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}_{h,2}

hold,

\mathbb{E}[D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}|\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}_{h-1,2}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}|\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}_{h,1}]=0
. Notice that
t(k,0,2)=t(k-1,H,2)
and
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{0,2}=\mathcal{F}^{(k-1)}_{H,2}
for
\forall k\geq 2
. Therefore,
one
can define
a
martingale sequence adapted to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{h,m}\}_{(k,h,m)\in[K]\times[H]\times[2]}
:

\displaystyle s^{(k+1)}_{h,j}=\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\sum_{h^{\prime}=0}^{H-1}\left(D^{k^{\prime}}_{h^{\prime},1}+D^{k^{\prime}}_{h^{\prime},2}\right)+\sum_{h^{\prime}=0}^{h}\left(D^{(k+1)}_{h^{\prime},1}+D^{(k+1)}_{h^{\prime},2}\right)+\sum_{(k^{\prime},h^{\prime},j)\in[K]\times[H]\times[2]}D^{k^{\prime}}_{h^{\prime},j}

Let

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\left(D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}+D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}\right)=S^{K-1}_{H,2}

Since
\gamma^{h}\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)},\gamma^{h+1}\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}\in[0,\hat{r}_{\text{max}}/(1-\gamma)]
and
\gamma^{h}Q_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)},\gamma^{h+1}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)},(k+1)}\in[0,r_{\text{max}}/(1-\gamma)]
, it holds that

|D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}|,~|D^{(k+1)}_{h,s}|\leq(r_{\text{max}}\vee\hat{r}_{\text{max}})/(1-\gamma)
for
\forall(k,h)\in[K-1]\times[H]
. Then, by the Azuma-Hoeffding inequlaity, the following inequality holds
:

\mathbb{P}\left(|S^{K-1}_{H,2}|\leq s\right)\geq 2\exp\left(\frac{-s^{2}}{16\left(\frac{r_{\text{max}}\vee\hat{r}_{\text{max}}}{1-\gamma}\right)^{2}\cdot(K-1)H}\right)

For any
p\in(0,1)
, if we set
s=4(r_{\text{max}}\vee\hat{r}_{\text{max}})(1-\gamma)^{-1}\sqrt{(K-1)H\log(4/p)}
, then the inequality holds with probability at least
1-p/2
.
The
term
3
can be bounded as

3

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\left(D^{(k+1)}_{h,1}+D^{(k+1)}_{h,2}\right)-\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\gamma^{h}\iota^{(k+1)}_{h}(s^{(k+1)}_{h},a^{(k+1)}_{h})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4(r_{\text{max}}\vee\hat{r}_{\text{max}})}{1-\gamma}\sqrt{(K-1)H\log(4/p)}-\iota^{K}_{H}

(D.35)

4. Upper bound
on
dynamic regret.

:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}\left(\{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}\}_{1:K-1},K)\right)

4.3. Upper bound of Theorem
1
.

Then, combining
4.1
,
4.2
provides the expression,

\mathfrak{R}\left(\{\widehat{\pi}^{(k+1)}\}_{1:K-1},K)\right)\leq\mathfrak{R}_{I}+\mathfrak{R}_{II}

where
\mathfrak{R}_{II}=\mathfrak{R}_{\texttt{Alg}}
if we use
Alg
as the baseline algorithm and
\mathfrak{R}_{II}=\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}
if we use
\mathfrak{R}_{\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}}
as the baseline algorithm:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{I}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\bar{\iota}_{\infty}^{K}-\iota^{(k)}_{H}+C_{p}\sqrt{K-1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\texttt{Alg}}=C_{\texttt{Alg}}(\Delta_{\pi})\cdot(K-1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}}=C_{\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}}(\Delta_{\pi})\cdot(K-1)

where the corresponding
constants are

\displaystyle C_{p}=\frac{4(r_{\text{max}}\vee\hat{r}_{\text{max}})}{1-\gamma}\sqrt{H\log(4/p)},~~C_{\texttt{Alg}}(\Delta_{\pi})=\left(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{\log{|\mathcal{A}|}}{\eta}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\Delta_{\pi}}+\frac{4\gamma^{H}(\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}\vee r_{\text{max}})}{1-\gamma}

\displaystyle C_{\texttt{Alg}_{\tau}}(\Delta_{\pi})=\left(\gamma+2\right)\left[(1-\eta\tau)^{\Delta_{\pi}-1}C_{1}+C_{2}\right]+\frac{4\gamma^{H}(\widehat{r}_{\text{max}}\vee r_{\text{max}})}{1-\gamma}+\frac{2\tau\log|\mathcal{A}|}{1-\gamma}

∎

\displaystyle:\Delta_{\pi}\geq\frac{\log\left(\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}(\gamma+2)}\right)}{\log(1-\eta\tau)}+1\quad\text{or}\quad\Delta_{\pi}\geq\frac{1}{\eta\tau}\log\left(\frac{C_{1}(\gamma+2)}{\epsilon}\right)+1

(D.39)

The inequalities (
D.37
)
and
(
D.39
)
results
from applying
the first-order
Taylor series on
\log(\gamma)

and

\log(1-\eta\tau)
since

\eta\in(0,(1-\gamma)/\tau]
. The
inequalities
(
D.36
)
and
(
D.39
) implies that if the learning rate
\eta
is fixed in the admissible range, then the iteration complexity scales inversely proportional
to

\tau
, and the upper bound
on

\delta
, which we will denote it as
\delta_{\text{max}}
, also scales proportional
to

\tau
.

Now, the
best guaranteed convergence can be

\eta^{*}=(1-\gamma)/\tau

(associated with the value of

that minimizes the equation (

))

and
